48:         "Reads the Excel file using a library (not hand-rolled CSV parsing)",
49:         "Correctly calculates profit margin from columns C and D",
50:         "Adds new column E with the calculated values",
51:         "Saves the file with the new column included"
52:       ]
53:     }
54:   ]
55: }
56: ```
57: 
58: **Note**: `failure_modes_without_skill` is documented separately in the design phase (see §Failure Modes to Document above) but is not part of the evals.json schema — it becomes the baseline for measuring skill lift.
59: ```
60: 
61: ## When to Use Eval-First
62: 
63: Evaluation-first is recommended when:
64: - Creating a skill for a new domain or workflow
65: - The skill has multiple distinct behaviors (needs to verify each)
66: - The user reports vague "it doesn't work" feedback
67: - Multiple developers will use the same skill
68: 
69: Evaluation-first is NOT needed when:
70: - Skill does one very simple thing (e.g., format conversion)
71: - User already has clear success criteria documented
72: - Skill is being used as a one-off prototype
73: 
74: ## Integration with Iteration Loop
75: 
76: After Step 0, evaluation cases become the regression test suite for future iterations:
77: - Each improvement cycle re-runs all eval cases
78: - Baseline is the previous iteration's output (or original without-skill output)
79: - Pass rate must improve or stay flat; regressions are flagged immediately
80: - New edge cases discovered during iteration are added to the eval set

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/improvement-workflows.md
1: # Improvement Workflows Reference
2: 
3: Purpose: The situational workflows of skill-creator-advance — quick-path testing, structural rewrite flow, the improvement iteration loop, and blind comparison — extracted from SKILL.md for token efficiency. SKILL.md keeps only routing pointers; this reference holds the full procedural detail.
4: 
5: ---
6: 
7: ## Do NOT Load Guards
8: 
9: **SKILL.md embedding instructions include explicit guards at these points:**
10: 
11: - **Quick eval path: Testing**: "Read only when running the Quick eval path."
12: - **Case (c) Structural Rewrite Flow**: "Read only when the improvement router identified case (c) — a structural change."
13: - **Improving the skill (iteration loop)**: "Read after presenting eval results, when turning user feedback into skill changes."
14: - **Advanced: Blind comparison**: "Read only when the user asks whether the new version is actually better."
15: 
16: ---
17: 
18: ## Quick eval path: Testing
19: 
20: For skills on the quick eval path, skip Step 0 (Evaluation-First) and test the draft directly: come up with 2-3 realistic test prompts — the kind of thing a real user would actually say — and share them with the user: "Here are a few test cases I'd like to try. Do these look right, or do you want to add more?" (not necessarily verbatim). Then run them. **For improvements to an existing skill, also run the old skill version on the same test cases as the baseline comparison** (snapshot before editing: `cp -r <skill-path> <workspace>/skill-snapshot/`); for brand-new skills, no baseline is needed.
21: 
22: Save test cases to `evals/evals.json`. Don't write assertions yet — just the prompts. You'll draft assertions later if you escalate to the full path.
23: 
24: ```json
25: {
26:   "skill_name": "example-skill",
27:   "evals": [
28:     {
29:       "id": 1,
30:       "prompt": "User's task prompt",
31:       "expected_output": "Description of expected result",
32:       "files": [],
33:       "expectations": [],
34:       "skip_reason": null
35:     }
36:   ]
37: }
38: ```
39: 
40: See `references/schemas.md` for the full schema (including the `expectations` field, which you'll add later).
41: 
42: **Quick-path iteration loop:** After improving the skill, rerun the 2-3 test cases manually — for improvements, include baseline comparison (run the old skill version on the same test cases); iterate on direct user feedback as in the original Quick eval path. Keep going until the user says they're happy, the feedback is all empty, or you're not making meaningful progress.
43: 
44: ---
45: 
46: ## Case (c): Structural Rewrite Flow
47: 
48: For case (c) — structural change (add / split / merge phases, change agent decomposition, change input/output contract) — use this flow (steps 1–4). Reuse existing eval cases and skip Step 0 when they exist. If absent, use the selected quick/full setup to create representative cases and run the old-version baseline before editing; snapshot the original first and retain it for comparison. These are new cases, not evidence of past tests. Cases (a) and (b) hand off to the dedicated sibling skills (`skill-refactor`, `skill-tuning`) and do **not** use these steps. Case (d) is handled by the Description Optimization workflow.
49: 
50: ### 1. Assess the Current State
51: 
52: Read the existing SKILL.md and all bundled files. Understand:
53: - What the skill does and how it's structured
54: - What conventions it follows (check the parent plugin's style)
55: - Known issues the user reports or you observe
56: 
57: ### 2. Diagnose Improvement Areas
58: 
59: Look at these dimensions (these apply to **structural** rewrites; they are not the right lens for token refactor or output A/B):
60: - **Triggering**: Is the description specific enough? Does it undertrigger or overtrigger?
61: - **Instructions**: Are they clear? Do they explain the "why"? Are there gaps?
62: - **Structure**: Is the directory organization appropriate for the skill's complexity?
63: - **Coverage**: Are edge cases handled? Are there missing workflows?
64: - **Bundled files**: Are reference files up to date? Are scripts working?
65: 
66: ### 3. Propose Changes
67: 
68: Present a concise improvement plan to the user before making changes. Group changes by impact:
69: - **High impact**: Changes that affect the skill's core behavior or triggering
70: - **Low impact**: Cleanup, reorganization, wording improvements
71: 
72: ### 4. Evaluate
73: 
74: Use the eval workflow (quick or full path, depending on complexity) to verify improvements. When improving an existing skill, the baseline should be the original version — snapshot it before editing:
75: 
76: ```bash
77: cp -r <skill-path> <workspace>/skill-snapshot/
78: ```
79: 
80: Then use the improvement iteration loop below.
81: 
82: ---
83: 
84: ## Improving the skill (the iteration loop)
85: 
86: The heart of the loop: turn the user's feedback on the test results into a better skill.
87: 
88: ### How to think about improvements
89: 
90: 1. **Generalize from the feedback.** You're creating a skill to be used a million times across many prompts; you iterate on a few examples only for speed — a skill that works only for those examples is useless. Rather than fiddly overfitty changes or oppressively constrictive MUSTs, when an issue is stubborn try branching out — different metaphors, different patterns of working. It's cheap to try and you might land on something great.
91: 
92: 2. **Keep the prompt lean.** Remove things that aren't pulling their weight. Read the **transcripts**, not just the final outputs — outputs tell you *what* happened, transcripts *how*. If the skill makes the model waste time on unproductive work, remove the parts causing it.
93: 
94: 3. **Explain the why.** Today's LLMs have good theory of mind and, given a good harness, go beyond rote instructions. Even if the user's feedback is terse or frustrated, understand why they wrote what they wrote and transmit that understanding into the instructions. If you find yourself writing ALWAYS or NEVER in all caps, that's a yellow flag — reframe and explain the reasoning so that the model understands why it matters.
95: 
96: 4. **Look for repeated work across test cases.** If the transcripts show subagents independently writing similar helper scripts or taking the same multi-step approach (all 3 test cases wrote a `create_docx.py`), that's a strong signal the skill should bundle that script: write it once, put it in `scripts/`, and tell the skill to use it.
97: 
98: ### The loop
99: 
100: After improving the skill:
101: 
102: 1. Apply your improvements to the skill
103: 2. **For Full eval path:** Rerun all test cases into a new `iteration-<N+1>/` directory, including baseline runs. If you're creating a new skill, the baseline is always `without_skill` (no skill) — that stays the same across iterations. If you're improving an existing skill, use the snapshot of the current version (taken before editing) as the baseline for all iterations. This ensures consistent comparison against the version being improved.
104: 3. **For Quick eval path:** Rerun the 2-3 test cases manually as described in the Quick eval path section above.
105: 4. Present results inline and save to `review.md`, noting changes from previous iteration
106: 5. Wait for the user to review and tell you they're done
107: 6. Read the new feedback, improve again, repeat
108: 
109: Keep going until:
110: - The user says they're happy
111: - The feedback is all empty (everything looks good)
112: - You're not making meaningful progress
113: 
114: ---
115: 
116: ## Advanced: Blind comparison
117: 
118: For a more rigorous comparison between two versions of a skill (the user asks "is the new version actually better?"), read `agents/comparator.md` and `agents/analyzer.md`: give two outputs to an independent agent without telling it which is which, let it judge quality, then analyze why the winner won. Optional, requires subagents; the human review loop is usually sufficient.
119: 
120: > **Boundary note vs `skill-dev-toolkit:skill-tuning`**: the blind comparator uses an LLM subagent as judge — fast and cheap, but inherits LLM-as-judge limitations (verbosity bias, position bias, weak signal on taste-sensitive output dimensions like voice / tone / creative quality). For taste-sensitive A/B that needs reliable preference signal, use `skill-tuning` instead — it uses **human** judgment per iteration and accumulates a preference log. Rule of thumb: blind comparator for objective / structured outputs (file transforms, code generation, fixed-format generators); `skill-tuning` for subjective / creative outputs (writing style, design feel, persuasive copy).

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/iteration-automation.md
1: # Iteration Automation
2: 
3: Utility functions for skill iteration workflow including regression detection, timing analysis, and improvement suggestions.
4: 
5: These are documented procedures that agents follow during evaluation — not an importable Python module. The function signatures describe the expected inputs and outputs for each analysis step.
6: 
7: ## Self-Assessment Protocol (Step 3.5)
8: 
9: Before grading, perform a quick automated check on each output:
10: 
11: 1. **Read each test case's output** and check for obvious defects:
12:    - Empty output
13:    - Format violations
14:    - Crash artifacts (stack traces, error messages in output)
15: 
16: 2. **If a defect is clearly caused by a skill instruction issue**:
17:    - Fix the skill
18:    - Rerun that test case once (quick rerun within the same iteration directory — this is an inline fix, not a new iteration)
19:    - Capture timing data for the rerun and save to `timing-rerun.json` in the run directory (NOT to the original `timing.json` — keep original for baseline comparison)
20:    - Log results to `self_assessment.json` in each test case directory
21:    - **Important:** This is a mid-iteration inline repair, not a new iteration. The self-assessment fix does NOT create a new iteration-<N+1> directory and does NOT require a new baseline snapshot. Only proceed to a new iteration (Step 4) if the self-assessment reveals a deeper skill design issue that requires a full rerun.
22: 
23: 3. **One pass only** — no infinite repair loops.
24: 
25: 4. **After self-assessment, proceed to Step 4 (Grade, aggregate)** — the iteration loop handles reruns of all test cases in a new directory when the full rerun is needed.
26: 
27: ## Regression Detection
28: 
29: Detects skill regressions by comparing pass rates between versions.
30: 
31: **Procedures:**
32: - **calculate_pass_rate_delta(current, baseline)** → Returns percentage point difference between current and baseline pass rates
33: - **is_significant_regression(delta, threshold=0.15)** → Returns True if delta < -threshold (meaningful degradation of 15% or more)
34: - **detect_flaky_eval(pass_rates)** → Returns True if stddev > 0.25 (high variance indicates flakiness)
35: 
36: **Usage in evaluation:**
37: When comparing iteration results, compute the pass rate delta. If `is_significant_regression(delta)` returns True, flag as regression and report to user.
38: 
39: ## Timing Analysis
40: 
41: Analyzes execution time trends and identifies performance regressions.
42: 
43: **Procedures:**
44: - **analyze_timing_trend(times)** → Returns {"trend": "improving"/"degrading"/"stable", "slope": float}
45: - **flag_performance_regression(current_time, baseline_mean, baseline_stddev)** → True if current > baseline_mean + 2*baseline_stddev
46: - **identify_outlier_cases(case_timings)** → Returns list of eval IDs with timing > Q3 + 1.5*IQR
47: 
48: ## Improvement Suggestions
49: 
50: Generates actionable suggestions based on benchmark analysis.
51: 
52: **Procedures:**
53: - **suggest_assertion_improvements(failed_assertions)** → Recommends how to make assertions more specific
54: - **suggest_doc_improvements(unclear_outputs)** → Recommends adding examples or constraints to description
55: - **suggest_tool_usage_changes(unexpected_tools)** → Flags tools used that aren't in allowed-tools list
56: 
57: ## Benchmark Aggregation Helpers
58: 
59: **Procedures:**
60: - **aggregate_run_summary(runs)** → Computes mean/stddev for pass_rate, time_seconds, tokens per configuration
61: - **calculate_improvement_delta(with_skill, without_skill)** → Returns improvement strings like "+0.50"
62: - **validate_benchmark_structure(data)** → Ensures benchmark.json follows schema before viewer consumption

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/mermaid-usage-guidelines.md
1: # Mermaid Usage Guidelines
2: 
3: Guidelines for when and how to use Mermaid diagrams in skill authoring.
4: 
5: ## When to Use
6: - **State machines / flows** — multi-step processes with branching
7: - **Architecture / data flow** — components and their connections
8: - **Sequence diagrams** — agent interactions, API calls, user journeys
9: - **Class / entity relationships** — when static structure matters
10: 
11: ## Guidelines
12: 1. **Prefer prose for simple cases** — A 3-step linear flow is clearer as a numbered list
13: 2. **Use Mermaid for complexity** — Branching, loops, parallel paths, or ≥4 components
14: 3. **Syntax conventions**:
15:    - Use `graph TD` for top-down flows
16:    - Use `sequenceDiagram` for interactions over time
17:    - Use `classDiagram` for type relationships
18:    - Label edges with verbs, not nouns
19: 4. **Cost-benefit framework**:
20:    - **Cost**: Rendering time, token overhead, maintenance burden
21:    - **Benefit**: Clarity for complex relationships, visual debugging
22:    - **Threshold**: Use Mermaid only when prose would take ≥2× the space or fail to show the relationship
23: 5. **Maintenance**: Diagrams must be regenerated when the described system changes — include a comment with the source file that generates the diagram if applicable
24: 
25: ## Quick Reference
26: | Diagram Type | Use Case | Syntax Start |
27: |---|---|---|
28: | Flow | Linear or branching processes | `graph TD` |
29: | Sequence | Agent/API/user interactions | `sequenceDiagram` |
30: | Class | Type hierarchies, data models | `classDiagram` |
31: | State | State machines | `stateDiagram-v2` |
32: | Gantt | Timelines, schedules | `gantt` |
33: | Pie | Proportions | `pie` |

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/platform-adaptations.md
1: # Platform Adaptations
2: 
3: Guidelines for adapting skills to different Claude interfaces.
4: 
5: ## Platform Differences
6: Different Claude interfaces have varying capabilities that affect skill design:
7: 
8: | Platform | Constraints | Adaptation Strategy |
9: |---|---|---|
10: | **Claude.ai / Web** | Full tool access, file upload, long context | Standard skill design, leverage all available tools |
11: | **Claude for Desktop** | Similar to web, but may have local file access differences | Test local file paths, consider OS-specific tools |
12: | **Claude.ai / Cowork** | No browser access, no subagent spawning in some modes | Pre-compute data, avoid real-time web requests, use provided context |
13: | **Claude CLI / API** | Programmatic access, no interactive prompts | Design for headless operation, use structured inputs/outputs |
14: | **Mobile / Limited interfaces** | Shorter context, touch-focused | Prioritize concise outputs, avoid complex multi-step workflows |
15: 
16: ## Adaptation Strategy
17: When adapting a skill for platform constraints:
18: 
19: ### Step 1: Identify Constraint
20: - Which platform(s) will this skill run on?
21: - What specific capabilities are missing or limited?
22: - Example: "No subagent spawning" means you cannot use Agent() calls
23: 
24: ### Step 2: Modify Approach
25: - Replace disallowed patterns with allowed alternatives
26: - Example: Instead of spawning subagents for parallel processing, use sequential processing with progress tracking
27: - Example: Instead of file upload/download, use clipboard or structured text exchange
28: 
29: ### Step 3: Update Documentation
30: - Add platform-specific notes in SKILL.md using conditional syntax
31: - Example: `[Claude.ai only: Use file upload]` or `[CLI only: Expect JSON input]`
32: - Update references/platform-adaptations.md with your specific findings
33: 
34: ### Step 4: Test
35: - Verify the skill works on all target platforms
36: - Document any remaining limitations in the skill description
37: 
38: ## Common Adaptations
39: - **No subagents**: Pre-compute data, use sequential processing
40: - **No browser**: Embed essential data, avoid real-time scraping
41: - **Shorter context**: Chunk inputs, summarize history
42: - **No file upload**: Use inline text, base64 small assets when essential
43: EOF

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/plugin-conventions.md
1: # Plugin Conventions
2: 
3: This file documents conventions for plugin structure, folder layout,
4: slash commands, and token budget management.
5: 
6: ## Folder Structure
7: - SKILL.md at root
8: - Single-level subdirectories only (references/, scripts/, agents/, etc.)
9: - No nested subdirectories
10: 
11: ## Slash Commands
12: Each skill should have a corresponding slash command entry point in the plugin's `commands/` directory.
13: 
14: ### Format
15: - File: `commands/<skill-name>.md` (or `commands/<plugin>/<skill-name>.md` for multi-skill plugins)
16: - Frontmatter required:
17:   ```yaml
18:   ---
19:   name: <skill-name>
20:   description: One-line description of what the skill does
21:   category: <skill-category>
22:   ---
23:   ```
24: - Body: Brief usage instructions, key triggers, and example invocations
25: 
26: ### Examples
27: **Simple skill:**
28: ```markdown
29: ---
30: name: json-to-yaml-converter
31: description: Converts JSON files when YAML output is needed
32: category: conversion
33: ---
34: 
35: # /json-to-yaml-converter
36: 
37: Convert JSON files to YAML format.
38: 
39: **Usage:** `/json-to-yaml-converter <input.json> [--output <output.yaml>]`
40: 
41: **Triggers:** "convert this JSON to YAML", "save as YAML"
42: ```
43: 
44: **Complex skill:**
45: ```markdown
46: ---
47: name: skill-creator-advance
48: description: Creates skills when a new capability needs an evaluation-first workflow
49: category: development
50: ---
51: 
52: # /skill-creator-advance
53: 
54: Create new skills using an evaluation-first development workflow.
55: 
56: **Usage:** `/skill-creator-advance <intent>`
57: 
58: **Triggers:** "build a skill", "make a slash command", "redesign [a skill]", "improve [a skill]"
59: ```
60: 
61: ## Token Budget
62: - SKILL.md under 5,000 tokens
63: - Progressive disclosure via Do NOT Load guards
64: - Reference files for content exceeding token limits
65: 
66: ## Progressive Disclosure
67: - Core skill logic stays the same
68: - Platform-specific adaptations in dedicated files
69: - Use conditional loading based on detected platform
70: 
71: ## SKILL.md Token Budget
72: - Target: ~5,000 tokens (~3,750 words) soft limit
73: - Extract detail to reference files behind Do NOT Load guards when approaching limit
74: - Each reference file: ~2,000-5,000 tokens depending on complexity
75: - Total skill package: ~25,000 tokens max for reliable context handling

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/pre-creation-gates.md
1: # Pre-Creation Gates Reference
2: 
3: Purpose: Extracted from SKILL.md for token efficiency. The main skill file keeps only routing pointers; this reference holds the full protocol.
4: 
5: ---
6: 
7: ## Do NOT Load Guards
8: 
9: **SKILL.md embedding instructions include explicit guards at these points:**
10: 
11: - **Quick eval path**: Read only §Gate 2 — Smallest-end-state check and answer its questions inline; skip the remaining gates and full interview.
12: - **Full creation workflow**: Load the complete protocol before Capture Intent.
13: 
14: ---
15: 
16: ## Phase 1/3: Initial Validation
17: 
18: Before intake / interview / drafting, run two lightweight gates against the user's request — one or two focused questions each; they prevent shipping a skill that should not have been built.
19: 
20: ### Gate 1 — Worth-it check
21: 
22: Applicable when the user proposes ≥2 skills at once, or one skill with multiple supporting claims ("we need this because A, B, and C"). Triage each proposed item into KEEP / DEFER / DROP, judging it on evidence grounding (is the need real and recurring, not speculative?) and YAGNI (would you build it now?).
23: 
24: A single skill with a single load-bearing reason keeps this gate cheap — confirm the reason holds, then move to Gate 2.
25: 
26: ### Gate 2 — Smallest-end-state check
27: 
28: Applicable to **every** new skill proposal, single or multi. The three questions:
29: 
30: 1. What's the smallest end state that solves this? (Could it be 0 functions — not really a skill, just a one-off prompt? One existing skill plus a new section instead of a new skill?)
31: 2. Does this result in less total skill-ecosystem code than not building it? (A new skill adds surface area; default "no" unless it subtracts other artifacts or replaces ad-hoc prompts.)
32: 3. What does this skill make obsolete? (If nothing, the rationale is purely additive — apply deletion-first skepticism as for any feature add.)
33: 
34: Skip explicitly if the user has already done the equivalent analysis ("we discussed this last week and concluded we need a dedicated skill"). Do NOT skip just to move faster — the cost of building the wrong skill is permanent.
35: 
36: If a gate verdict is DROP / REJECT / RESHAPE, surface it and ask the user how to proceed (drop, defer, or reshape smaller) before continuing to intake.
37: 
38: ### Gate 3 — User-input check (after intake, before drafting)
39: 
40: Does this skill have any user-input branching? If yes, plan to apply the hardened `AskUserQuestion` pattern from `references/asking-user-questions.md` when drafting the relevant STEP — without it, the skill is highly likely to inline-fallback or silently default in production. If no user input is needed, this gate is N/A.

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/process-pattern.md
1: # Process Pattern Reference
2: 
3: **Process pattern.** This skill follows the Process pattern: every skill it produces must have a clear lifecycle — create → test → evaluate → improve → repeat.
4: 
5: A skill for creating new skills and iteratively improving them.
6: 
7: The core loop: **draft → test → review → improve → repeat**.
8: 
9: - Decide what the skill should do → write a draft
10: - Create test prompts → run claude-with-the-skill on them
11: - Evaluate results (qualitative review + quantitative assertions)
12: - Rewrite based on feedback → repeat until satisfied
13: - Optionally optimize the description for better triggering
14: 
15: Your job is to figure out where the user is in this process and help them progress — from scratch or from an existing draft. Be flexible: if they say "just vibe with me", skip the formal eval machinery.
16: 
17: ---
18: 
19: **Usage:** This reference contains the core process pattern explanation extracted from SKILL.md for token efficiency. SKILL.md keeps only routing pointers; this reference holds the full procedural detail.
20: 
21: **When to read:** Read this when you need the full lifecycle framing (create → test → evaluate → improve → repeat). SKILL.md carries a condensed version inline.

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/schemas.md
1: # JSON Schemas
2: 
3: This document defines the JSON schemas used by skill-creator.
4: 
5: ---
6: 
7: ## evals.json
8: 
9: Defines the evals for a skill. Located at `evals/evals.json` within the skill directory.
10: 
11: ```json
12: {
13:   "skill_name": "example-skill",
14:   "evals": [
15:     {
16:       "id": 1,
17:       "prompt": "User's example prompt",
18:       "expected_output": "Description of expected result",
19:       "files": ["evals/files/sample1.pdf"],
20:       "expectations": [
21:         "The output includes X",
22:         "The skill used script Y"
23:       ]
24:     }
25:   ],
26:   "skip_reason": "Quick path: skill is a simple format conversion with clear success criteria"
27: }
28: ```
29: 
30: **Fields:**
31: - `skill_name`: Name matching the skill's frontmatter
32: - `evals[].id`: Unique integer identifier
33: - `evals[].prompt`: The task to execute
34: - `evals[].expected_output`: Human-readable description of success
35: - `evals[].files`: Optional list of input file paths (relative to skill root)
36: - `evals[].expectations`: List of verifiable statements
37: - `skip_reason`: Optional string explaining why the quick eval path was chosen instead of the full path (e.g., "simple skill with clear success criteria", "one-off prototype")
38: 
39: ---
40: 
41: ## eval_metadata.json
42: 
43: Metadata for a single evaluation case within an iteration. Located at `<workspace>/iteration-<N>/eval-<ID>/eval_metadata.json`. This file is shared across configurations and repeat runs of that case; pass its path explicitly to each grader rather than copying it into run directories.
44: 
45: ```json
46: {
47:   "eval_id": 0,
48:   "eval_name": "descriptive-name-here",
49:   "prompt": "The user's task prompt",
50:   "assertions": []
51: }
52: ```
53: 
54: **Fields:**
55: - `eval_id`: Integer identifier for this eval case within the iteration
56: - `eval_name`: Human-readable descriptive name (used as directory name)
57: - `prompt`: The exact task prompt to execute
58: - `assertions`: Array of string assertions to verify (can be empty initially, filled during evaluation)
59: 
60: **Relationship to evals.json:** eval_metadata.json is per-test-case metadata for a specific run iteration. evals.json is the master list of all eval cases for the skill. The `assertions` field in eval_metadata.json corresponds to `expectations` in evals.json — use `assertions` during active evaluation runs and sync to `expectations` in evals.json when finalizing.
61: 
62: **Relationship to grading.json:** The grader reads `assertions` from eval_metadata.json and produces `expectations` (array of objects with `text`, `passed`, `evidence`) in grading.json. The assertion text from eval_metadata.json becomes the `text` field in grading.json expectations.
63: 
64: ---
65: 
66: ## grading.json
67: 
68: Output from the grader agent. Located at `<run-dir>/grading.json`.
69: 
70: **Source of assertions:** The grader reads `assertions` from the explicitly supplied path to the case's `eval_metadata.json` (see the eval_metadata.json section above). The assertion text strings become the `text` field in the grading.json `expectations` array. After grading, each expectation object has `text` (the original assertion), `passed` (boolean), and `evidence` (supporting detail).
71: 
72: ```json
73: {
74:   "expectations": [
75:     {
76:       "text": "The output includes the name 'John Smith'",
77:       "passed": true,
78:       "evidence": "Found in transcript Step 3: 'Extracted names: John Smith, Sarah Johnson'"
79:     },
80:     {
81:       "text": "The spreadsheet has a SUM formula in cell B10",
82:       "passed": false,
83:       "evidence": "No spreadsheet was created. The output was a text file."
84:     }
85:   ],
86:   "summary": {
87:     "passed": 2,
88:     "failed": 1,
89:     "total": 3,
90:     "pass_rate": 0.67
91:   },
92:   "execution_metrics": {
93:     "tool_calls": {
94:       "Read": 5,
95:       "Write": 2,
96:       "Bash": 8
97:     },
98:     "total_tool_calls": 15,
99:     "total_steps": 6,
100:     "errors_encountered": 0,
101:     "output_chars": 12450,
102:     "transcript_chars": 3200
103:   },
104:   "timing": {
105:     "executor_duration_seconds": 165.0,
106:     "grader_duration_seconds": 26.0,
107:     "total_duration_seconds": 191.0
108:   },
109:   "claims": [
110:     {
111:       "claim": "The form has 12 fillable fields",
112:       "type": "factual",
113:       "verified": true,
114:       "evidence": "Counted 12 fields in field_info.json"
115:     }
116:   ],
117:   "user_notes_summary": {
118:     "uncertainties": ["Used 2023 data, may be stale"],
119:     "needs_review": [],
120:     "workarounds": ["Fell back to text overlay for non-fillable fields"]
121:   },
122:   "eval_feedback": {
123:     "suggestions": [
124:       {
125:         "assertion": "The output includes the name 'John Smith'",
126:         "reason": "A hallucinated document that mentions the name would also pass"
127:       }
128:     ],
129:     "overall": "Assertions check presence but not correctness."
130:   }
131: }
132: ```
133: 
134: **Fields:**
135: - `expectations[]`: Graded expectations with evidence
136: - `summary`: Aggregate pass/fail counts
137: - `execution_metrics`: Tool usage and output size (from executor's metrics.json)
138: - `timing`: Wall clock timing (from timing.json)
139: - `claims`: Extracted and verified claims from the output
140: - `user_notes_summary`: Issues flagged by the executor
141: - `eval_feedback`: (optional) Improvement suggestions for the evals, only present when the grader identifies issues worth raising
142: 
143: ---
144: 
145: ## metrics.json
146: 
147: Output from the executor agent. Located at `<run-dir>/outputs/metrics.json`.
148: 
149: ```json
150: {
151:   "tool_calls": {
152:     "Read": 5,
153:     "Write": 2,
154:     "Bash": 8,
155:     "Edit": 1,
156:     "Glob": 2,
157:     "Grep": 0
158:   },
159:   "total_tool_calls": 18,
160:   "total_steps": 6,
161:   "files_created": ["filled_form.pdf", "field_values.json"],
162:   "errors_encountered": 0,
163:   "output_chars": 12450,
164:   "transcript_chars": 3200
165: }
166: ```
167: 
168: **Fields:**
169: - `tool_calls`: Count per tool type
170: - `total_tool_calls`: Sum of all tool calls
171: - `total_steps`: Number of major execution steps
172: - `files_created`: List of output files created
173: - `errors_encountered`: Number of errors during execution
174: - `output_chars`: Total character count of output files
175: - `transcript_chars`: Character count of transcript
176: 
177: ---
178: 
179: ## timing.json
180: 
181: Wall clock timing for a run. Located at `<run-dir>/timing.json`.
182: 
183: **How to capture:** Save observed task-notification fields immediately when available; hosts may omit `total_tokens` or `duration_ms`, and ephemeral telemetry may not be recoverable. Compute seconds only from measured duration. If notification duration is absent, use a finite, nonnegative numeric measurement from an identified source and disclose it; if none is available, disclose unavailable timing and stop the full timing comparison (quality may be presented separately). The legacy duration path defaults missing values to zero and does not support null; never write invented zero or null duration into it.
184: 
185: `total_tokens` must be a finite, nonnegative JSON number; a captured `0` is valid. Missing files, malformed JSON or non-object timing data, and absent or invalid `total_tokens` mean token capture is unavailable. Do not substitute output character counts or zero for unavailable measurements. Duration precedence is unchanged: use grading timing first, then sibling timing when grading duration is zero.
186: 
187: ```json
188: {
189:   "total_tokens": 84852,
190:   "duration_ms": 23332,
191:   "total_duration_seconds": 23.3,
192:   "executor_start": "2026-01-15T10:30:00Z",
193:   "executor_end": "2026-01-15T10:32:45Z",
194:   "executor_duration_seconds": 165.0,
195:   "grader_start": "2026-01-15T10:32:46Z",
196:   "grader_end": "2026-01-15T10:33:12Z",
197:   "grader_duration_seconds": 26.0
198: }
199: ```
200: 
201: ---
202: 
203: ## benchmark.json
204: 
205: Output from Benchmark mode. Located at `benchmarks/<timestamp>/benchmark.json`.
206: 
207: ```json
208: {
209:   "metadata": {
210:     "skill_name": "pdf",
211:     "skill_path": "/path/to/pdf",
212:     "executor_model": "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
213:     "analyzer_model": "most-capable-model",
214:     "timestamp": "2026-01-15T10:30:00Z",
215:     "evals_run": [1, 2, 3],
216:     "runs_per_configuration": 3
217:   },
218: 
219:   "runs": [
220:     {
221:       "eval_id": 1,
222:       "eval_name": "Ocean",
223:       "configuration": "with_skill",
224:       "run_number": 1,
225:       "result": {
226:         "pass_rate": 0.85,
227:         "passed": 6,
228:         "failed": 1,
229:         "total": 7,
230:         "time_seconds": 42.5,
231:         "tokens": 3800,
232:         "tool_calls": 18,
233:         "errors": 0
234:       },
235:       "expectations": [
236:         {"text": "...", "passed": true, "evidence": "..."}
237:       ],
238:       "notes": [
239:         "Used 2023 data, may be stale",
240:         "Fell back to text overlay for non-fillable fields"
241:       ]
242:     }
243:   ],
244: 
245:   "run_summary": {
246:     "with_skill": {
247:       "pass_rate": {"mean": 0.85, "stddev": 0.05, "min": 0.80, "max": 0.90},
248:       "time_seconds": {"mean": 45.0, "stddev": 12.0, "min": 32.0, "max": 58.0},
249:       "tokens": {"mean": 3800, "stddev": 400, "min": 3200, "max": 4100, "sample_count": 3, "total_count": 3}
250:     },
251:     "without_skill": {
252:       "pass_rate": {"mean": 0.35, "stddev": 0.08, "min": 0.28, "max": 0.45},
253:       "time_seconds": {"mean": 32.0, "stddev": 8.0, "min": 24.0, "max": 42.0},
254:       "tokens": {"mean": 2100, "stddev": 300, "min": 1800, "max": 2500, "sample_count": 3, "total_count": 3}
255:     },
256:     "delta": {
257:       "pass_rate": "+0.50",
258:       "time_seconds": "+13.0",
259:       "tokens": "+1700"
260:     }
261:   },
262: 
263:   "notes": [
264:     "Assertion 'Output is a PDF file' passes 100% in both configurations - may not differentiate skill value",
265:     "Eval 3 shows high variance (50% ± 40%) - may be flaky or model-dependent",
266:     "Without-skill runs consistently fail on table extraction expectations",
267:     "Skill adds 13s average execution time but improves pass rate by 50%"
268:   ]
269: }
270: ```
271: 
272: **Fields:**
273: - `metadata`: Information about the benchmark run
274:   - `skill_name`: Name of the skill
275:   - `timestamp`: When the benchmark was run
276:   - `evals_run`: List of eval names or IDs
277:   - `runs_per_configuration`: Observed runs per eval/configuration when all counts are equal (e.g. 3, not a default); `0` when no runs were loaded, or `null` when counts differ. Counts include zero for an observed eval missing a discovered configuration; see `runs` for individual observations.
278: - `runs[]`: Individual run results
279:   - `eval_id`: Numeric eval identifier
280:   - `eval_name`: Human-readable eval name (used as section header in the viewer)
281:   - `configuration`: Must be `"with_skill"`, `"without_skill"`, or `"old_skill"` (the viewer uses this exact string for grouping and color coding: with_skill, without_skill, and old_skill)
282:   - `run_number`: Integer run number (1, 2, 3...)
283:   - `result`: Nested object with `pass_rate`, `passed`, `total`, `time_seconds`, `tokens`, `errors`
284:     - `tokens`: Captured numeric total (including `0`), or `null` when capture is unavailable.
285: - `run_summary`: Statistical aggregates per configuration
286:   - `with_skill` / `without_skill`: Each contains `pass_rate`, `time_seconds`, `tokens` objects with `mean` and `stddev` fields
287:     - `tokens`: `mean`, `stddev`, `min`, and `max` use captured observations only, and are all `null` when none are captured, including an empty configuration. `sample_count` counts captured totals; `total_count` counts loaded runs. Their ratio describes coverage, so partial statistics are not whole-population measurements.
288:   - `delta`: Difference strings like `"+0.50"`, `"+13.0"`, `"+1700"`. The `tokens` delta is `null` unless both compared configurations have at least one run and complete token capture; partial capture never supports a numeric saving.
289: - `notes`: Freeform observations from the analyzer
290: 
291: In benchmark Markdown, unavailable token statistics display `not captured (0/N)` and partial statistics include `partial: M/N captured`. An unavailable token delta displays `not captured`. Fully captured numeric formatting, pass-rate statistics, and duration statistics are unchanged.
292: 
293: **Important:** The viewer reads these field names exactly. Using `config` instead of `configuration`, or putting `pass_rate` at the top level of a run instead of nested under `result`, will cause the viewer to show empty/zero values. Always reference this schema when generating benchmark.json manually.
294: 
295: ---
296: 
297: ## comparison.json

300:

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/skill-writing-guide.md
1: # Skill Writing Guide Reference
2: 
3: Purpose: Extracted from SKILL.md for token efficiency. The main skill file keeps only routing pointers; this reference holds the structural conventions an agent follows when planning and writing a new skill's layout.
4: 
5: ---
6: 
7: ## Do NOT Load Guards
8: 
9: **SKILL.md embedding instructions include explicit guards at these points:**
10: 
11: - **Plan the Skill Structure step**: Load when planning a new skill's file layout
12: - **Skip if**: You are only optimizing an existing skill's description or making minor tweaks
13: 
14: ---
15: 
16: ## Anatomy of a Skill
17: 
18: ```
19: skill-name/
20: ├── SKILL.md (required)
21: │   ├── YAML frontmatter (name, description required)
22: │   └── Markdown instructions
23: └── Bundled Resources (optional, single-level subdirectories ONLY)
24:     ├── scripts/    - Executable code for deterministic/repetitive tasks
25:     ├── references/ - Docs loaded into context as needed
26:     ├── assets/     - Templates / fixtures / static files
27:     ├── agents/     - Sub-agent definitions
28:     └── ... (any other single-level subdirectory you need)
29: ```
30: 
31: ## CRITICAL: Folder structure must be flat — NO nested subdirectories
32: 
33: Anthropic skill convention forbids subdirectories inside subdirectories under a skill root. A skill may contain `SKILL.md` plus any number of single-level subdirectories; those subdirectories must themselves be flat.
34: 
35: ```
36: ✅ CORRECT:
37: skill-name/SKILL.md
38: skill-name/scripts/extract_lineage.py
39: skill-name/references/spec.md
40: skill-name/assets/template.md
41: 
42: ❌ FORBIDDEN (will fail validation hooks in repos that enforce):
43: skill-name/assets/scripts/extract_lineage.py     ← assets/ contains scripts/
44: ```
45: 
46: ## When creating a new skill or adding bundled resources to an existing one:
47: 
48: - Group files by **subdirectory at skill root** (scripts/, assets/, references/, agents/, etc.) — choose meaningful names
49: - Inside each subdirectory, use **descriptive filenames** (e.g., `extract_column_lineage.py`) rather than further nesting
50: - If you feel the need to subdivide, extract into a new top-level subdirectory at the skill root instead (e.g., `scripts-redshift/` rather than `scripts/redshift/`)
51: 
52: ## Each skill should also have a corresponding **slash command** entry point in the plugin's `commands/` directory — format and examples in `references/plugin-conventions.md`.
53: 
54: ## Progressive Disclosure
55: 
56: Skills use three-level loading — metadata (always in context), SKILL.md body (loaded on trigger; keep under ~5,000 tokens / ~3,750 words, extracting detail into reference files when approaching the limit), bundled resources (as needed). Details: `references/plugin-conventions.md` (§Progressive Disclosure, §SKILL.md Token Budget).
57: 
58: **Key patterns:**
59: - Reference files clearly from SKILL.md with guidance on when to read them
60: - For large reference files (>~8,000 tokens), include a table of contents
61: 
62: **Domain organization**: When a skill supports multiple domains/frameworks, organize by variant (e.g., `references/aws.md`, `references/gcp.md`) — Claude reads only the relevant reference file.
63: 
64: ## Working with Existing Plugin Ecosystems
65: 
66: When creating a skill that will live inside an existing plugin, match the plugin's conventions rather than imposing a new structure — read `references/plugin-conventions.md` (observing the target plugin's style, the lightweight/standard/full structure spectrum, key conventions).
67: 
68: ## Principle of Lack of Surprise
69: 
70: This goes without saying, but skills must not contain malware, exploit code, or any content that could compromise system security. A skill's contents should not surprise the user in their intent if described. Don't go along with requests to create misleading skills or skills designed to facilitate unauthorized access, data exfiltration, or other malicious activities. Things like a "roleplay as an XYZ" are OK though.
71: 
72: ## Empty-Prompt Onboarding
73: 
74: When drafting a skill that can be invoked with no prompt or a very sparse one, read `references/asking-user-questions.md` §Empty-Prompt Onboarding for the opt-in "surface orientation" pattern (recommended for conversational / multi-workflow skills; single-shot utility skills are exempt).
75: 
76: ## Asking the User Structured Questions (when to use AskUserQuestion)
77: 
78: When a skill needs user input mid-execution that's a discrete choice between 2-4 options (e.g., "which folders to exclude?"), use the `AskUserQuestion` tool — but apply the **hardened pattern** documented in `references/asking-user-questions.md`. The naive approach (`Use AskUserQuestion to confirm...` with a fenced Q&A template) fails three documented modes (inline fallback, silent default, tool unavailable); the reference closes all three and includes a copy-paste mandatory-gate template.
79: 
80: Always-included for skills with user-input steps. Skills with no user-input steps are exempt — don't add AskUserQuestion just because it exists.
81: 
82: ## Writing Patterns
83: 
84: Prefer the imperative form in instructions.
85: 
86: **Defining output formats** — for example:
87: ```markdown
88: ## Report structure
89: ALWAYS use this exact template:
90: # [Title]
91: ## Executive summary
92: ## Key findings
93: ## Recommendations
94: ```
95: 
96: **Examples pattern** - Examples are useful. Format them like this (deviate a little if "Input"/"Output" already appear in them):
97: ```markdown
98: ## Commit message format
99: **Example 1:**
100: Input: Added user authentication with JWT tokens
101: Output: feat(auth): implement JWT-based authentication
102: ```
103: 
104: ## Writing Style
105: 
106: Explain to the model why things matter instead of heavy-handed MUSTs. Keep the skill general, not narrowed to specific examples. Draft first, then revisit with fresh eyes and improve.
107: 
108: For writing lean from the start — token economy, bloat self-review, thin-orchestrator design — read `references/writing-lean.md`.

FILE skill-creator-advance/references/writing-lean.md
1: # Writing Lean
2: 
3: Token economy guidelines for skill documentation.
4: 
5: ## Principles
6: - Prefer concise instructions over verbose explanations
7: - Use structured formats (bulleted lists, tables) over prose
8: - Extract detail to reference files behind Do NOT Load guards
9: - Target SKILL.md under 5,000 tokens (soft limit)
10: 
11: ## Techniques
12: - Use references for deep dives
13: - Compress examples to minimal working cases
14: - Avoid repeating information across files

FILE skill-creator-advance/scripts/__init__.py


FILE skill-creator-advance/scripts/aggregate_benchmark.py
1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
2: """
3: Aggregate individual run results into benchmark summary statistics.
4: 
5: Reads grading.json files from run directories and produces:
6: - run_summary with mean, stddev, min, max for each metric
7: - delta between with_skill and without_skill configurations
8: 
9: Usage:
10:     python aggregate_benchmark.py <benchmark_dir>
11: 
12: Example:
13:     python aggregate_benchmark.py benchmarks/2026-01-15T10-30-00/
14: 
15: The script supports two directory layouts:
16: 
17:     Workspace layout (from skill-creator iterations):
18:     <benchmark_dir>/
19:     └── eval-N/
20:         ├── with_skill/
21:         │   ├── run-1/grading.json
22:         │   └── run-2/grading.json
23:         └── without_skill/
24:             ├── run-1/grading.json
25:             └── run-2/grading.json
26: 
27:     Legacy layout (with runs/ subdirectory):
28:     <benchmark_dir>/
29:     └── runs/
30:         └── eval-N/
31:             ├── with_skill/
32:             │   └── run-1/grading.json
33:             └── without_skill/
34:                 └── run-1/grading.json
35: """
36: 
37: import argparse
38: import json
39: import math
40: import sys
41: from datetime import datetime, timezone
42: from pathlib import Path
43: 
44: 
45: def calculate_stats(values: list[float]) -> dict:
46:     """Calculate mean, stddev, min, max for a list of values."""
47:     if not values:
48:         return {"mean": 0.0, "stddev": 0.0, "min": 0.0, "max": 0.0}
49: 
50:     n = len(values)
51:     mean = sum(values) / n
52: 
53:     if n > 1:
54:         variance = sum((x - mean) ** 2 for x in values) / (n - 1)
55:         stddev = math.sqrt(variance)
56:     else:
57:         stddev = 0.0
58: 
59:     return {
60:         "mean": round(mean, 4),
61:         "stddev": round(stddev, 4),
62:         "min": round(min(values), 4),
63:         "max": round(max(values), 4)
64:     }
65: 
66: 
67: def load_run_results(benchmark_dir: Path) -> dict:
68:     """
69:     Load all run results from a benchmark directory.
70: 
71:     Returns dict keyed by config name (e.g. "with_skill"/"without_skill",
72:     or "new_skill"/"old_skill"), each containing a list of run results.
73:     """
74:     # Support both layouts: eval dirs directly under benchmark_dir, or under runs/
75:     runs_dir = benchmark_dir / "runs"
76:     if runs_dir.exists():
77:         search_dir = runs_dir
78:     elif list(benchmark_dir.glob("eval-*")):
79:         search_dir = benchmark_dir
80:     else:
81:         print(f"No eval directories found in {benchmark_dir} or {benchmark_dir / 'runs'}")
82:         return {}
83: 
84:     results: dict[str, list] = {}
85: 
86:     for eval_idx, eval_dir in enumerate(sorted(search_dir.glob("eval-*"))):
87:         metadata_path = eval_dir / "eval_metadata.json"
88:         if metadata_path.exists():
89:             try:
90:                 with open(metadata_path) as mf:
91:                     eval_id = json.load(mf).get("eval_id", eval_idx)
92:             except (json.JSONDecodeError, OSError):
93:                 eval_id = eval_idx
94:         else:
95:             try:
96:                 eval_id = int(eval_dir.name.split("-")[1])
97:             except ValueError:
98:                 eval_id = eval_idx
99: 
100:         # Discover config directories dynamically rather than hardcoding names
101:         for config_dir in sorted(eval_dir.iterdir()):
102:             if not config_dir.is_dir():
103:                 continue
104:             # Skip non-config directories (inputs, outputs, etc.)
105:             if not list(config_dir.glob("run-*")):
106:                 continue
107:             config = config_dir.name
108:             if config not in results:
109:                 results[config] = []
110: 
111:             for run_dir in sorted(config_dir.glob("run-*")):
112:                 run_number = int(run_dir.name.split("-")[1])
113:                 grading_file = run_dir / "grading.json"
114: 
115:                 if not grading_file.exists():
116:                     print(f"Warning: grading.json not found in {run_dir}")
117:                     continue
118: 
119:                 try:
120:                     with open(grading_file) as f:
121:                         grading = json.load(f)
122:                 except json.JSONDecodeError as e:
123:                     print(f"Warning: Invalid JSON in {grading_file}: {e}")
124:                     continue
125: 
126:                 # Extract metrics
127:                 result = {
128:                     "eval_id": eval_id,
129:                     "run_number": run_number,
130:                     "pass_rate": grading.get("summary", {}).get("pass_rate", 0.0),
131:                     "passed": grading.get("summary", {}).get("passed", 0),
132:                     "failed": grading.get("summary", {}).get("failed", 0),
133:                     "total": grading.get("summary", {}).get("total", 0),
134:                 }
135: 
136:                 # Extract timing — check grading.json first, then sibling timing.json
137:                 timing = grading.get("timing", {})
138:                 result["time_seconds"] = timing.get("total_duration_seconds", 0.0)
139:                 result["tokens"] = None
140:                 timing_file = run_dir / "timing.json"
141:                 if timing_file.exists():
142:                     try:
143:                         with open(timing_file) as tf:
144:                             timing_data = json.load(tf)
145:                         if not isinstance(timing_data, dict):
146:                             timing_data = {}
147:                         if result["time_seconds"] == 0.0:
148:                             result["time_seconds"] = timing_data.get("total_duration_seconds", 0.0)
149:                         tokens = timing_data.get("total_tokens")
150:                         if type(tokens) in (int, float) and math.isfinite(tokens) and tokens >= 0:
151:                             result["tokens"] = tokens
152:                     except json.JSONDecodeError:
153:                         pass
154: 
155:                 # Extract metrics if available
156:                 metrics = grading.get("execution_metrics", {})
157:                 result["tool_calls"] = metrics.get("total_tool_calls", 0)
158:                 result["errors"] = metrics.get("errors_encountered", 0)
159: 
160:                 # Extract expectations — viewer requires fields: text, passed, evidence
161:                 raw_expectations = grading.get("expectations", [])
162:                 for exp in raw_expectations:
163:                     if "text" not in exp or "passed" not in exp:
164:                         print(f"Warning: expectation in {grading_file} missing required fields (text, passed, evidence): {exp}")
165:                 result["expectations"] = raw_expectations
166: 
167:                 # Extract notes from user_notes_summary
168:                 notes_summary = grading.get("user_notes_summary", {})
169:                 notes = []
170:                 notes.extend(notes_summary.get("uncertainties", []))
171:                 notes.extend(notes_summary.get("needs_review", []))
172:                 notes.extend(notes_summary.get("workarounds", []))
173:                 result["notes"] = notes
174: 
175:                 results[config].append(result)
176: 
177:     return results
178: 
179: 
180: def aggregate_results(results: dict) -> dict:
181:     """
182:     Aggregate run results into summary statistics.
183: 
184:     Returns run_summary with stats for each configuration and delta.
185:     """
186:     run_summary = {}
187:     configs = sorted(results, key=lambda config: config != "with_skill")
188: 
189:     for config in configs:
190:         runs = results.get(config, [])
191: 
192:         pass_rates = [r["pass_rate"] for r in runs]
193:         times = [r["time_seconds"] for r in runs]
194:         tokens = [r["tokens"] for r in runs if r.get("tokens") is not None]
195:         token_stats = calculate_stats(tokens) if tokens else dict.fromkeys(
196:             ("mean", "stddev", "min", "max"), None
197:         )
198:         token_stats.update(sample_count=len(tokens), total_count=len(runs))
199: 
200:         run_summary[config] = {
201:             "pass_rate": calculate_stats(pass_rates),
202:             "time_seconds": calculate_stats(times),
203:             "tokens": token_stats
204:         }
205: 
206:     # Keep the current skill before its baseline; retain other discovery order.
207:     if len(configs) >= 2:
208:         primary = run_summary.get(configs[0], {})
209:         baseline = run_summary.get(configs[1], {})
210:     else:
211:         primary = run_summary.get(configs[0], {}) if configs else {}
212:         baseline = {}
213: 
214:     delta_pass_rate = primary.get("pass_rate", {}).get("mean", 0) - baseline.get("pass_rate", {}).get("mean", 0)
215:     delta_time = primary.get("time_seconds", {}).get("mean", 0) - baseline.get("time_seconds", {}).get("mean", 0)
216:     token_a = primary.get("tokens", {})
217:     token_b = baseline.get("tokens", {})
218:     complete_tokens = all(
219:         stats.get("sample_count", 0) > 0
220:         and stats["sample_count"] == stats["total_count"]
221:         for stats in (token_a, token_b)
222:     )
223:     delta_tokens = token_a["mean"] - token_b["mean"] if complete_tokens else None
224: 
225:     run_summary["delta"] = {
226:         "pass_rate": f"{delta_pass_rate:+.2f}",
227:         "time_seconds": f"{delta_time:+.1f}",
228:         "tokens": f"{delta_tokens:+.0f}" if delta_tokens is not None else None
229:     }
230: 
231:     return run_summary
232: 
233: 
234: def generate_benchmark(benchmark_dir: Path, skill_name: str = "", skill_path: str = "") -> dict:
235:     """
236:     Generate complete benchmark.json from run results.
237:     """
238:     results = load_run_results(benchmark_dir)
239:     run_summary = aggregate_results(results)
240: 
241:     # Build runs array for benchmark.json
242:     runs = []
243:     for config in results:
244:         for result in results[config]:
245:             runs.append({
246:                 "eval_id": result["eval_id"],
247:                 "configuration": config,
248:                 "run_number": result["run_number"],
249:                 "result": {
250:                     "pass_rate": result["pass_rate"],
251:                     "passed": result["passed"],
252:                     "failed": result["failed"],
253:                     "total": result["total"],
254:                     "time_seconds": result["time_seconds"],
255:                     "tokens": result.get("tokens"),
256:                     "tool_calls": result.get("tool_calls", 0),
257:                     "errors": result.get("errors", 0)
258:                 },
259:                 "expectations": result["expectations"],
260:                 "notes": result["notes"]
261:             })
262: 
263:     # Determine eval IDs from results
264:     eval_ids = sorted(set(
265:         r["eval_id"]
266:         for config in results.values()
267:         for r in config
268:     ))
269:     repetition_counts = {
270:         sum(r["eval_id"] == eval_id for r in config_runs)
271:         for eval_id in eval_ids
272:         for config_runs in results.values()
273:     }
274:     runs_per_configuration = (
275:         next(iter(repetition_counts)) if len(repetition_counts) == 1
276:         else None if repetition_counts else 0
277:     )
278: 
279:     benchmark = {
280:         "metadata": {
281:             "skill_name": skill_name or "<skill-name>",
282:             "skill_path": skill_path or "<path/to/skill>",
283:             "executor_model": "<model-name>",
284:             "analyzer_model": "<model-name>",
285:             "timestamp": datetime.now(timezone.utc).strftime("%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ"),
286:             "evals_run": eval_ids,
287:             "runs_per_configuration": runs_per_configuration
288:         },
289:         "runs": runs,
290:         "run_summary": run_summary,
291:         "notes": []  # To be filled by analyzer
292:     }
293: 
294:     return benchmark
295: 
296: 
297: def generate_markdown(benchmark: dict) -> str:
298:     """Generate human-readable benchmark.md from benchmark data."""
299:     metadata = benchmark["metadata"]
300:     run_summary = benchmark["run_summary"]
301: 
302:     # Determine config names (excluding "delta")
303:     configs = sorted((k for k in run_summary if k != "delta"),
304:                      key=lambda config: config != "with_skill")
305:     config_a = configs[0] if len(configs) >= 1 else "config_a"
306:     config_b = configs[1] if len(configs) >= 2 else "config_b"
307:     label_a = config_a.replace("_", " ").title()
308:     label_b = config_b.replace("_", " ").title()
309:     repetitions = metadata["runs_per_configuration"]
310:     population = (
311:         f"{repetitions} runs each per configuration" if repetitions is not None
312:         else "unequal run counts per eval/configuration; see runs"
313:     )
314: 
315:     lines = [
316:         f"# Skill Benchmark: {metadata['skill_name']}",
317:         "",
318:         f"**Model**: {metadata['executor_model']}",
319:         f"**Date**: {metadata['timestamp']}",
320:         f"**Evals**: {', '.join(map(str, metadata['evals_run']))} ({population})",
321:         "",
322:         "## Summary",
323:         "",
324:         f"| Metric | {label_a} | {label_b} | Delta |",
325:         "|--------|------------|---------------|-------|",
326:     ]
327: 
328:     a_summary = run_summary.get(config_a, {})
329:     b_summary = run_summary.get(config_b, {})
330:     delta = run_summary.get("delta", {})
331: 
332:     # Format pass rate
333:     a_pr = a_summary.get("pass_rate", {})
334:     b_pr = b_summary.get("pass_rate", {})
335:     lines.append(f"| Pass Rate | {a_pr.get('mean', 0)*100:.0f}% ± {a_pr.get('stddev', 0)*100:.0f}% | {b_pr.get('mean', 0)*100:.0f}% ± {b_pr.get('stddev', 0)*100:.0f}% | {delta.get('pass_rate', '—')} |")
336: 
337:     # Format time
338:     a_time = a_summary.get("time_seconds", {})
339:     b_time = b_summary.get("time_seconds", {})
340:     lines.append(f"| Time | {a_time.get('mean', 0):.1f}s ± {a_time.get('stddev', 0):.1f}s | {b_time.get('mean', 0):.1f}s ± {b_time.get('stddev', 0):.1f}s | {delta.get('time_seconds', '—')}s |")
341: 
342:     # Format tokens
343:     def format_tokens(summary):
344:         stats = summary.get("tokens", {})
345:         captured, total = stats.get("sample_count", 0), stats.get("total_count", 0)
346:         if not captured:
347:             return f"not captured (0/{total})"
348:         text = f"{stats['mean']:.0f} ± {stats['stddev']:.0f}"
349:         if captured < total:
350:             text += f" (partial: {captured}/{total} captured)"
351:         return text
352: 
353:     lines.append(f"| Tokens | {format_tokens(a_summary)} | {format_tokens(b_summary)} | {delta.get('tokens') or 'not captured'} |")
354: 
355:     # Notes section
356:     if benchmark.get("notes"):
357:         lines.extend([
358:             "",
359:             "## Notes",
360:             ""
361:         ])
362:         for note in benchmark["notes"]:
363:             lines.append(f"- {note}")
364: 
365:     return "\n".join(lines)
366: 
367: 
368: def main():
369:     parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
370:         description="Aggregate benchmark run results into summary statistics"
371:     )
372:     parser.add_argument(
373:         "benchmark_dir",
374:         type=Path,
375:         help="Path to the benchmark directory"
376:     )
377:     parser.add_argument(
378:         "--skill-name",
379:         default="",
380:         help="Name of the skill being benchmarked"
381:     )
382:     parser.add_argument(
383:         "--skill-path",
384:         default="",
385:         help="Path to the skill being benchmarked"
386:     )
387:     parser.add_argument(
388:         "--output", "-o",
389:         type=Path,
390:         help="Output path for benchmark.json (default: <benchmark_dir>/benchmark.json)"
391:     )
392: 
393:     args = parser.parse_args()
394: 
395:     if not args.benchmark_dir.exists():
396:         print(f"Directory not found: {args.benchmark_dir}")
397:         sys.exit(1)
398: 
399:     # Generate benchmark
400:     benchmark = generate_benchmark(args.benchmark_dir, args.skill_name, args.skill_path)
401: 
402:     # Determine output paths
403:     output_json = args.output or (args.benchmark_dir / "benchmark.json")
404:     output_md = output_json.with_suffix(".md")
405: 
406:     # Write benchmark.json
407:     with open(output_json, "w") as f:
408:         json.dump(benchmark, f, indent=2)
409:     print(f"Generated: {output_json}")
410: 
411:     # Write benchmark.md
412:     markdown = generate_markdown(benchmark)
413:     with open(output_md, "w") as f:
414:         f.write(markdown)
415:     print(f"Generated: {output_md}")
416: 
417:     # Print summary
418:     run_summary = benchmark["run_summary"]
419:     configs = [k for k in run_summary if k != "delta"]
420:     delta = run_summary.get("delta", {})
421: 
422:     print(f"\nSummary:")
423:     for config in configs:
424:         pr = run_summary[config]["pass_rate"]["mean"]
425:         label = config.replace("_", " ").title()
426:         print(f"  {label}: {pr*100:.1f}% pass rate")
427:     print(f"  Delta:         {delta.get('pass_rate', '—')}")
428: 
429: 
430: if __name__ == "__main__":
431:     main()

FILE skill-creator-advance/scripts/generate_report.py

Each row is an iteration — a new description attempt. The columns show test queries: green checkmarks mean the skill triggered correctly (or correctly didn't trigger), red crosses mean it got it wrong. The "Train" score shows performance on queries used to improve the description; the "Test" score shows performance on held-out queries the optimizer hasn't seen. When it's done, Claude will apply the best-performing description to your skill.

164:         <p><strong>Iterations:</strong> {data.get('iterations_run', 0)} | <strong>Train:</strong> {data.get('train_size', '?')} | <strong>Test:</strong> {data.get('test_size', '?')}</p>
165:     </div>

173:         <span class="legend-item"><span class="legend-swatch swatch-negative"></span> Should NOT trigger</span>
174:         <span class="legend-item"><span class="legend-swatch swatch-train"></span> Train</span>
175:         <span class="legend-item"><span class="legend-swatch swatch-test"></span> Test</span>
176:     </div>
177: """)

180:     html_parts.append("""
